It follows the previous discussion on how the deceased is identified with Osiris to navigate the afterlife.</meta>

Vowel chanting is also found in Egyptian religious practice as reported by **Demetrius** in his Roman treatise, *On Style* <note>original Latin title: *De Elocutione* (OCR reads "De Eloutione"); a Greek work on literary composition likely from the 2nd century BC</note>:

> "In Egypt the priests, when singing hymns in praise of the gods, employ the 7 vowels which they utter in due succession and the sound of these letters is so euphonious <note>pleasing to the ear</note> that men listen to it in place of the flute and lyre"

The distinction between religion and magic in scholarly discourse breaks down in the context of Egyptian religion and it is reasonable to suspect that vowel chanting could be used for more than hymns of praise by Egyptian priests.

Thus, self-identification with deity and use of a specific kind of magical language found in the <term>PGM</term> <note>Greek Magical Papyri: a body of papyri from Graeco-Roman Egypt containing spells, recipes, and rituals</note> places Egyptian magical notions within a Greek magical context. The question then becomes, can evidence be found that Greek magic, prior to the <term>PGM</term>, included these practices and do they appear in later Greek magical material that we know to have influenced the European tradition.

**Hans Dieter Betz** <note>A prominent New Testament scholar and expert on the PGM</note> states in the *Encyclopedia of Religion* that "magic was an essential part of Greco-Roman culture and religion." In classical Greece, Egypt and **Thessaly** <note>A region in Greece famously associated with powerful witches and herbal magic in antiquity</note> were considered prime sources of magical knowledge, but by 323 BC <note>The death of Alexander the Great, marking the start of the Hellenistic period</note> magical material in Greece had increased considerably. Betz further states that it was "Hellenistic <term>syncretism</term> <note>the blending or merging of different religions, cultures, or schools of thought</note> that produced the abundance of material available today." Greek magical practitioners distinguished different types of magic: <term>sorcery</term> <note>original: *goeteia* — often viewed as lower magic or common witchcraft</note>, <term>magic</term> <note>original: *mageia* — a general term for the magical arts</note> and <term>theurgy</term> <note>original: *theourgia* — "god-working," high magic intended to invoke or unite with the divine</note>. <term>Theurgy</term> appears to be the most likely place to find self-identification with deity and the use of *<term>magical words</term>* <note>original: *voces magicae* — strings of powerful, often untranslatable names or sounds used in ancient spells</note>.

<summary>This page explores the connection between Egyptian vowel chanting and Greek magical practices, specifically how the blending of these cultures led to the high magic of theurgy. It highlights how practitioners used specific sounds and self-identification with gods to exert influence over the divine and material worlds.</summary>
<keywords>vowel chanting, Demetrius, Greek Magical Papyri (PGM), Hans Dieter Betz, Hellenistic syncretism, goeteia, mageia, theurgy, voces magicae, religious history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Greek Magical Papyri (PGM), shifting from the use of vowel chanting to a comparison of how Greek and Egyptian cultures viewed the relationship between humans and the divine.</meta>

Self-identification with deity in magical acts as part of ancient Greek magical practice prior to the <term>PGM</term> <note>Greek Magical Papyri: a collection of magical texts from Graeco-Roman Egypt</note> is not evident. The Greeks speculated that humans and gods "had the same mother" <note>A likely reference to the poet Pindar, who noted that gods and men share a common origin despite the vast difference in their power</note>, but a huge gap existed between them. From ancient times to the latest date of the <term>PGM</term>, Greek notions about the relationship between human existence and divine existence took a variety of forms but never followed the Egyptian pattern of the possibility of declarative divine identity <note>The ritual practice of a person stating "I am [God's name]" to exert authority</note>. The ancient Greeks believed that communion with the gods was possible as in the **Eleusian** and **Dionysian mysteries** <note>Secret religious rites of ancient Greece associated with the afterlife and divine ecstasy</note> and **Empedocles** <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who famously claimed to be an immortal god among men</note> declared he had the knowledge to make himself immortal. But, the Greek idea of a divine spark within the human soul which can be activated, contemplated and re-united with the gods still assumes an other-ness of deity and validates the fundamental separateness of human existence from the divine.

For the Egyptians, the divine appears to be immanent <note>inherent or dwelling within the physical world</note> in the world. The world of humans and gods were not seen as being decidedly different. Human activity continued after death and Gods, embodied as the **Pharaoh**, lived in human society. Magical practice was merely clarifying what already exists. For the Greeks, magic was a conduit for communication and communion with deity or a process whereby the soul could be purified through direct contact with the Divine. Egyptians had only to affirm a state of being through speech to create the sought reality. "Repeated commands or assertions that a desired state of affairs was already in being, are a common feature of Egyptian spells."

However, there are references to the *magical words* <note>original: *voces magicae*; these are powerful, often "nonsensical" syllables or secret names used in rituals</note> in ancient Greek material aside from the <term>PGM</term>. Early, are the **Ephesian letters** <note>original: *Ephesia grammata*; a famous set of six magical words used in Greek amulets and protective charms</note>, (**ASKION**, **KATASKION**, **LIX**, **TETRAX**,

It introduces the 'Ephesian letters' as early Greek evidence of magical language used outside the PGM tradition.</summary>

DAMNAMENEUS, AISIA <note>Two of the six traditional "Ephesia grammata," or Ephesian letters</note> are mystic letters that were supposedly inscribed on the statue of <term>Artemis at Ephesus</term> <note>One of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World; the goddess Artemis here was a unique, multi-breasted figure representing fertility and nature</note> used verbally and written to avert evil. This notion appears close to the Egyptian ideas that words are not symbols, but realities.

Fox argues that the <term>PGM</term> <note>The Greek Magical Papyri, a collection of magical handbooks from late antiquity</note> are carrying forward this "shamanic" tradition of magical musical charms. For the actual author(s) of the <term>PGM</term>, the notion of the magical potency of language could have been very strong indeed coming out of both the Egyptian and Greek magical traditions.

<term>Magic words</term> <note>original: "Voces magicae"; strings of syllables or secret names believed to have inherent ritual power</note> are also referred to in the <term>Chaldean Oracles</term> <note>a 2nd-century mystical text that combined Platonic philosophy with ritual practice</note> which are contemporary with the <term>Greek Magical Papyri</term> <note>often abbreviated PGM; a vast collection of ancient spells and rituals found in Egypt</note> and they appear to be an intrinsic part of the <term>theurgist's</term> <note>from "theurgy," meaning "god-working"—the practice of using rituals to communicate with or ascend to the divine</note> ritual. What is intriguing, for this study, about the Chaldean Oracles, is the relationship between the magic words and the process of immortalization of the soul, which is the goal of theurgy. These texts provide the closest approximation to self-identification with deity in a non-Egyptian context. According to the Chaldeans, the soul, in its descent to the body gathers impure substances. Through theurgistic rites, the soul can re-ascend, encounter the Divine and be purified of these impure substances and attain immortality. The magic words invoke the assistant spirits that will help the soul to ascend without fear of being dragged down into <term>Hades</term> <note>the underworld or the realm of the dead</note>. However, even though immortalization is the goal, self-identification with deity is not declared and only the soul can attain such a state.

The idea that the Egyptian language specifically held magical power is seen in the writings of people of the time. In the <term>Hermetica</term> <note>a collection of wisdom texts attributed to the legendary sage Hermes Trismegistus</note> (CH xvi) there is a passage which states that Greeks will not understand the Hermetica when translated into their language as Greek does not contain the power of Egyptian. The Chaldean Oracles state "do not ever alter the foreign names (of the gods)". <term>Hans Lewy</term> <note>a 20th-century scholar of ancient religion and philosophy</note> elaborates further, "It is impossible to translate the magical formula, because its power it *not* due to its external sense." <term>Iamblichus</term> <note>a Neoplatonist philosopher who defended the use of ritual and sacred names</note>, describing the difficulty of translating the Hermetica from Egyptian to Greek says ". . .for the very quality of the sounds

# The Demons of Magic

I was first asked to talk about demons of magic and the notion was that I would show slides representing various demonic beings, demons we find on the magical gems. It occurred to me that you were probably familiar with them. You certainly would be if you looked at the works placed on the admirable bibliography that was circulated, especially Bonner's *Studies in Magical Amulets, Chiefly Graeco-Egyptian* is to be highly recommended. If you do look at those you will find that the imagination of the Greco-Near Easterners of the second century A.D. produced a fantastic population of beings who are quite unlike ancient near Eastern gods, on the one hand, and even less like the standard Greek or Roman gods, on the other. As far as iconography <note>the visual images and symbols used in a work of art or the study of these symbols</note> goes there was a flourishing or a remarkable growth of new and fantastic forms. This did not by any means take care of all the gods that were used in magic. I will come back to that point presently.

I decided not to deal with this question because I thought, as I have said, that if you were reading the recommended bibliography or if you were interested in the purely iconographic side of magical invention, you could easily do that for yourselves. A friend of mine who gave up church history for history of art told me, "I used to have to read the texts but now I just have to look at the pictures." For iconographic studies you could do that; one picture is worth a thousand words. Besides that, I was having trouble as I started preparing for that since the magical gems commonly show the gods that are invoked, how do you know when something that appears on a magical gem is a god, and when is it a <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "daimon"; in antiquity, this referred to a supernatural power or lesser deity that could be good or evil, distinct from the high gods of the pantheon</note>. Take the

...rooster-headed <term>anguipede</term> <note>The OCR reads "angleped," but this refers to the "anguipede," a creature with snakes for legs, from the Latin *anguis* (snake) and *pes* (foot)</note>, for instance. He is a rooster from the neck up, and from the neck to the knees he is a Roman soldier in regular Roman soldier's armor. He grasps a good round Roman shield, and wields the whip. And from the knees down he is a pair of serpents. An odd creature. Very widely represented, very often with the name Abrasax, which led to a belief that he was a representation of Abrasax. But Iao <note>A Greek form of the Hebrew name for God, Yahweh</note> also appears very often in connection with him. So it was thought too that he was a representation of Iao. You had your choice, and you could solve the problem by saying that this was a representation of Abrasax-Iao or Iao-Abrasax, a product of <term>syncretism</term> <note>the blending or merging of different religions, cultures, or schools of thought</note>. So I decided that I was facing a new problem, or a problem that I had not seen adequately studied. And that is what exactly is the usage of the term <term>daimon</term> <note>a Greek word for a spirit or divine power; the ancestor of the English word "demon"</note>, and related terms? You know of course that the <term>Septuagint</term> <note>the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> has a very simple and brief answer to that, all the gods of the nations are demons <note>original Greek: *apantes hoi theoi tōn ethnōn daimonia*</note>,

To Homer this statement would have been unobjectionable. Homer knows that gods are <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> and Nock <note>Arthur Darby Nock (1902–1963), a renowned scholar of Greek and Roman religion</note> argued this in *Classical Philology* 45 49, with references to many previous discussions. "Spirit-beings" <note>original Greek: *daimonia*</note> is just the derived adjective from the spirits <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> so that's no problem. But

<meta>This page continues the analysis of divine terminology, specifically investigating the hierarchy of supernatural beings in ancient thought. What is wrong is that the <term>Septuagint</term> <note>the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, traditionally dated to the 3rd century BCE</note> didn't intend to make a statement of fact; that statement is <term>polemic</term> <note>a strong verbal or written attack on someone's opinions or beliefs</note>. The reader is intended to understand that, contrary to Homer, <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> are not gods. <term>Spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> are inferior beings. And the Septuagint, by saying that all the gods of the gentiles are <term>lesser spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimonia*</note>, is degrading them to a class of beings subordinate or inferior to the one true God.

When the Septuagint does this, it was not doing something that was radically new or peculiarly Jewish. The subordination of <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> to <term>gods</term> <note>original Greek: *theoi*</note> has classical precedents. One thinks immediately of Plato's *Apology*, of course. But Plato is by no means the only one nor is he the first. So you are faced with this fact that in the classical tradition is double. On the one hand, there is a tradition from Homer on down equating <term>spirit</term> <note>original Greek: *daimon*</note> with <term>god</term> <note>original Greek: *theos*</note>. On the other hand, there is a tradition dating back to the <term>pre-Socratic philosophers</term> <note>Greek thinkers active before the time of Socrates, often focused on the nature of the cosmos</note> subordinating <term>spirit</term> to <term>god</term>.

The question is, how did the magicians deal with this problem of second-class supernatural citizens? In the <term>papyri</term> <note>ancient manuscripts written on material made from the papyrus plant</note>, first of all you must remember that the bulk of papyri comes from the fourth and fifth centuries A.D.; they were written at that time, and certainly contain in some instances considerably older materials and in some instances contains materials invented by the writer. The safest thing to conclude is that you have materials from the fourth to the fifth century, in general. Sometimes one can see what looks like significant changes but it's hard to be sure. For instance, in the <term>Great Magical Papyrus of Paris</term> <note>a massive ancient handbook of spells and rituals, one of the most important sources for the study of Greco-Egyptian magic</note>, <term>spirit</term> <note>original Greek: *daimon*</note> is used pretty consistently all the way through until the last couple of hundred lines. Then <term>lesser spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimonia*</note> comes in and <term>spirit</term> disappears. It looks as if you had an appendix or at least as if the last sections of that papyrus were written by someone who

ancient evidence is quite small. And there are a lot of points against it besides that use of the <term>lesser spirit</term> <note>original Greek: *daimonion*</note>.

I haven't examined all of the ancient gems, of course. But this judgment is based on a concordance of them that was prepared by Mrs. Francis Schwartz, who examined about twenty of the major and minor published collections and a half dozen standard works on ancient gems. So we can leave the gems out of the question. The people who made them may have thought that they were representing <term>lesser spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimonia*</note>, but they never happened to say so, and we can't go confidently beyond their silence. In the <term>curse tablets</term> <note>original Latin: *defixiones*; these were small sheets of lead inscribed with spells intended to "bind" or hex an opponent, then buried in graves or wells</note> you run into another question, but I'll come back to the curse tablets in just a moment.

The papyri give you the fullest description of the <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> and their place in the world. They refer to them fairly often, as a class of beings who are intermediate, apparently, between gods and men. They are supernatural beings, in the sense that they haven't got human physical location and limitations, but they are subordinate to the gods. They are found in the air, on the earth, in the waters, and on the sea, and also in the underworld. An especially important class of them are the demons of the underworld, particularly the demons of the dead who become <term>spirits</term> <note>original: *daimones*</note> after death—apparently all the dead do, and whenever you have a dead man you have a <term>spirit of the dead</term> <note>original Greek: *nekudaimon*, likely transcribed here as *decidaimon*</note> who can be called up if you have some remains of the body, the proper spells, and so on. The <term>spirit of the dead</term> will be particularly effective if the dead man was killed violently, especially if he was executed for a crime, but also if he died young, particularly if he died before marriage. Those who did not reach their time of flourishing, and those who died as infants, especially,

<meta>This page continues the speaker’s analysis of spirits in ancient texts, specifically moving from archaeological artifacts like gems and curse tablets to the functional role of spirits as "helpers" or "laborers" within the Greek Magical Papyri.</meta>

They provide a great deal of demonic service for the practitioners of magic. For the most part, all of these <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> represent what you might call the "help" or the labor force of the magical world. They are summoned to provide various services for the magician. For example:

> "Go to a specific house and stand next to someone; take on the appearance of the god or spirit that he or she particularly reveres, and tell the target person the following."

Then you give the message you want conveyed. Or:

> "Take control of them,"

This instruction is usually used in attempts to attract women.

> "Make her jump out of bed and come to me right away and pound on the door."

> "Inflame her with passion. Make her burn."

And so forth and so on. You can also change the gender, but women, on the whole, are the more common targets in these manuscripts. The magical world depicted here is predominantly heterosexual, so charms of this sort are usually intended for men trying to reach women. You can also send out spirits to commit murder, or for all sorts of other purposes, such as to obtain information. I suppose that if magic were still a common practice today, there would be spells like:

> "Go to my competitor's computer and read what he has stored on the following keys."

So these spirits—what I might call the lower-class help or the "helper class"—frequently appear in lists, especially when the text discusses the <term>Great Name</term> <note>The secret, powerful name of a supreme deity, believed to grant the speaker authority over all of creation</note>.

> "I possess the Great Name, at which the gods prostrate themselves, the spirits are terrified, the wild animals take flight, and rivers flow backwards..."

The list continues from there. You normally start with the gods, then the spirits, then humans or wild animals, and finally other physical phenomena, such as the calming of the seas.

This makes up the great majority of references to <term>spirits</term> <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> in the <term>Magical Papyri</term> <note>A diverse collection of ancient Egyptian scrolls containing spells and rituals, mostly written in Greek</note>. Their appearances vary, but I do not think it is worthwhile to give specific figures because they are not very informative, seeing as the papyri are—

This isn't enough to make them by any means a major concern. They are very apt to be used when you have a spell for a purpose. You may, in many cases, use demons to carry out the purpose, but you may not. I think the majority of cases, probably the bulk, well I'd say a small but substantial majority of cases demons don't function; the magic is done directly by the power of the name or by knowledge of secret names or in most cases by the action of the god you call on. And even when there are demons, in a great majority of cases they function merely as obedient to the name of the god which you have, or to the commands which are authorizations that the god has given to you. This concept appears again in the <term>PGM<note>The Papyri Graecae Magicae, a collection of ancient Greek magical texts found in Egypt.</note></term> 12.130f. "And I say also to you because I have . . " (the magician is telling the deity he is speaking to him)

and so on. I think these examples suffice to show the problem that you have here, and I suspect it may be to a considerable extent a literary problem. In other words, the early tradition of Homer—where spirits <note>original Greek: *daimones*</note> were seen as gods—remained influential due to the importance of **Homer** in classical education. This lived on side by side with the developing and increasingly powerful classification of spirits as subordinate or lesser beings. And since magic is a matter of *ad hoc* spells rather than a systematic way of thinking, it is not surprising that you get survivals and mixed forms of these various different lines and stages of earlier thought.

The application of the term spirit <note>original Greek: *daimon*</note> to the greater gods is relatively limited. **Apollo** is called a spirit. The good spirit <note>original Greek: *agathos daimon*</note>, of course, is one. **Selene** <note>The personification of the Moon.</note>, especially when she is being called on to do unpleasant things, and **Octinofus** <note>Likely a variant of a magical name for Hecate or a solar-lunar deity.</note>, with whom she is identified, are called spirits. The use is occasional. **Seth** <note>An Egyptian god associated with storms, chaos, and the desert.</note> is a shady deity despite being described as a brilliant god. You can see the auto-suggestion of the magician, "Let the flame be open, ... let me see the depth, the breadth and the depth ..." and so on. But I am inclined to believe that four-dimensional thought is a modern phenomenon and what you have here is simple, old-fashioned rhetoric. <meta>This page continues the comparison between the Epistle to the Ephesians and ancient magical spells (specifically the Greek Magical Papyri), focusing on the shared motifs of divine indwelling, spiritual strength, and the "fullness" of God, concluding a discussion on the nature of ancient spirits.</meta>

The magical text goes on to say, ". . . Give your strength and arouse your <term>daimon <note>In the ancient world, a "daimon" was not necessarily an evil "demon," but a divine spirit or intermediary power that could inspire or protect an individual.</note></term> to be strengthened by his spirit and show me your power." The Book of Ephesians has, "And let the house of the all-ruling God be opened to cause Christ to dwell by means of faith in your hearts in order that you may understand with all the saints what is the length and depth ..." and so on. Ephesians says ". . in order that you may be filled with all of the <term>fullness <note>original Greek: plērōma. A technical term in early Christian and Gnostic thought referring to the totality of divine powers or the "completeness" of God’s being.</note></term> of God." 

And the magical text says, "And may the lord <term>Bouel <note>An Egyptian solar deity frequently invoked in magical papyri to bring light or reveal hidden truths.</note></term> who is within shine forth." It is clearly not a word-for-word derivation. These are two representatives of a single tradition which has the same essential thoughts in it but has been cast independently in two different sets of words. Nevertheless, they preserve the same body of topics in roughly the same order. Since this is done by arousing the god who is entreated to arouse his <term>daimon</term> in order to do this, I think that makes a fair ending for this talk about <term>daimones <note>The plural form of daimon; the speaker is concluding a lecture on these spiritual entities.</note></term>.
